After each choice, there is a certain probability (volatility rate) that whichever option was worth fewer points will increase by 20, "leapfrogging" the previously higher-valued option. The goal is to select the option worth more points as frequently as possible by deciding how often to sample the unknown option (exploration) versus remain with the known option (exploitation).

LF has both an anchor version and an AIG version. The anchor version consists of 200 trials in which

the option values are fixed for all participants based on a previously chosen random set of outcomes with a volatility rate of 12.5%. The AIG version is randomly generated for each participant independently with an equivalent volatility rate and number of trials.

In the computational cognitive psychology literature, LF is typically used to compare individual participants' choice patterns to different model-based decision algorithms, to determine which models best approximate human behavior. It may be possible to use these model-based approaches to generate scoring rules; however, for simplicity, we scored participants based on the proportion of trials in which they selected the higher option. The intuition behind the inclusion of LF is that knowing how and when to explore new sources of information rather than exploit familiar ones is a skill that could plausibly benefit forecasting proficiency.

Impossible Question Criterion (IQC).

The IQC (Bennett and Steyvers 2022; see also Bereby-Meyer et al. 2003) is made up of a series of general knowledge questions with two possible answers. For most questions, only one of the two answers is factually correct. For the remaining questions, there is no factually correct answer (impossible questions). For each question, participants can choose to opt out or answer the question. If they do answer the question, a confidence rating is also elicited. The test provides scores for the number of correct answers to the general knowledge questions (GK), judgmental calibration for the confidence judgments (C), and the number of times they chose to opt out of impossible questions (IQ).

The original IQC item bank consists of 100 questions, including six impossible questions. We generated a shortened anchor version of the IQC using 24 of the questions with correct answers, as well as the six impossible questions. We then created two additional AIG versions by splitting 48 of the remaining questions from the original item bank in half and supplementing each half with six newly generated impossible questions. Impossible questions were generated by ChatGPT. See Appendix D for a list of the newly generated questions and the prompt used to generate them.

The intuition behind the inclusion of the IQC is that knowing the extent of one's knowledge and having well-calibrated confidence are skills that could plausibly benefit forecasting proficiency.

Denominator Neglect (DN). DN was inspired by a common decision bias of the same name. Participants make a series of choices between two proportions with the goal of selecting the larger proportion.

Research indicates that people will tend to erroneously favor proportions that have larger numerators, even when they represent smaller proportions (Stanovich and West 2008). Inclusion of visual arrays can reduce denominator neglect (Garcia-Retamero et al. 2010).

Each DN item features a choice between two boxes with a mix of gold and silver coins. The goal is to select the box with a larger proportion of gold coins. One box always contains 10 total coins (the small denominator option) with either one, two, or three gold coins. The other box always has 400 total coins (the large denominator option), with proportions ranging from plus (harmony trials) or minus (conflict trials) 1% to 8% the small denominator option in intervals of 1%.

There are two different designs of DN: separate (S) and combined (C).

In the separate design, options have only a verbal description or a visual array, but not both. Items can either be heterogeneous (one option verbal and the other visual) or homogeneous (both options verbal or both options visual). In the combined design, each option is represented by both a verbal description and a visual array. Both designs have an anchor and an AIG version. The anchor version of the separate design consists of a single set of randomly generated items. The AIG version randomizes whether and how each item will be homogeneous or heterogeneous independently for each participant. The anchor version of the combined design consists of a fixed set of randomly generated small denominator option proportions (10%, 20%, and 30%), and the AIG version randomly generates these proportions independently for each participant. Each design of DN has 48 items. DN is scored as the total number of cases in which the larger proportion is selected.

Success on DN is driven by how well one understands the importance of total population sizes, or base rates, when judging event outcomes. The intuition behind the inclusion of DN is that being aware of the importance of base rates and their implications for making probabilistic decisions is a skill that could plausibly benefit forecasting proficiency.

Bayesian Updating (BU).

BU is based on experiments by Phillips and Edwards (1966) in which participants must iteratively update their probabilistic judgments based on new information. Two boxes are displayed: one containing p red balls and 100 -p blue balls, the other containing p blue balls and 100 -p red balls, with p randomly set to either 60 or 70. At the start of each trial, one of the boxes is chosen at random with equal probability, but it is not revealed to participants which box it is. Five balls are then drawn from the chosen box, one at a time, with replacement. After the fifth draw, the task resets, and a new trial begins.

The test has two designs, easy and hard, each of which consists of eight trials with five sequential decisions.

In the easy design, after each ball is drawn, participants report the probability that the chosen box is the majority blue box. In the hard design, participants report the probability that the next draw will be a blue ball. Each design also has an AIG and an anchor version. In the anchor version, the sequence of draws is fixed for all participants, while in the AIG version, the draws are randomly generated for each participant independently.

BU scores are based on the absolute deviation between the estimate made in each trial and the optimal Bayesian estimate (see Appendix G for scoring details). The intuition behind the inclusion of BU is that following normative rules about how and how much to update one's probabilistic beliefs is a skill that could plausibly benefit forecasting proficiency.

Adult Decision-Making Competence: Resistance to Framing (RF). The Adult Decision-Making

Competence (ADMC) inventory is a battery of tests designed to assess decision-making ability (Bruine de Bruin et al. 2007). Though the ADMC is well validated, to our knowledge it has not previously been included in any forecasting studies.

RF is one of three subscales we considered for the FPT. The RF subscale consists of a series of comparisons between two options. Each comparison is repeated at two different points during the ADMC: once in a positive frame and again in a negative frame. For each comparison, participants rate their preference for the options on a scale of 1 to 6, with 1 representing extreme preference for the first option and 6 extreme preference for the second option.

Scores are based on consistency in judgment across the positively and negatively framed versions of each comparison. The ADMC-RF was adapted exactly as described in Bruine de Bruin et al. (2007), with the positively and negatively framed versions of each comparison split between the two cognitive test blocks.

The intuition behind the inclusion of ADMC-RF is that resisting the effects of irrelevant information, such as how information is framed, is a skill that could plausibly benefit forecasting proficiency.

Adult Decision-Making Competence: Risk Consistency (RC).

The RC subscale of the ADMC is similar to one of the CFS testlets: it assesses the consistency of risk assessments across timeframes.

Participants are asked to assess the probability that several events occur within a series of increasing time horizons. For each event, they first rate the probability that it will occur within one year. Later in the test, the same events are repeated, except participants must report the probability that they will occur within three years. The same events are later repeated once more with a time horizon of five years. Judgments are considered consistent when the probabilities monotonically increase for longer time horizons.

The ADMC-RC was adapted exactly as described in Bruine de Bruin et al. (2007). The intuition behind the inclusion of ADMC-RC is that following the normative rules of probability judgment is a skill that is known to benefit forecasting accuracy, and this test operationalizes that in a slightly different way than the CFS.

Adult Decision-Making Competence: Decision Rules (DR).

In the DR subscale of the ADMC, participants must assess how consumers should make purchasing decisions based on a set of stated preferences.

Each consumer specifies how they prioritize a set of feature ratings for a product they wish to purchase.

Participants must then decide which of several options best satisfies the consumer's preferences.

The ADMC-DR was adapted exactly as described in Bruine de Bruin et al. (2007), with one small modification: the product targets were televisions rather than DVD players. The intuition behind the inclusion of the ADMC-DR is that being able to optimize judgments based on an set of explicitly stated rules is a skill that could plausibly benefit forecasting proficiency.

Graph Literacy Scale (GL).

GL is designed to assess how well subjects make inferences from visual representations of data (Galesic and Garcia-Retamero 2011). It was originally developed in the context of medical decision-making and to our knowledge has not been previously validated in the context of a forecasting study. The test was adapted exactly as described in Galesic and Garcia-Retamero (2011). The intuition behind the inclusion of GL is that being able to make inferences based on data presented in a graphical format is a skill that could plausibly benefit forecasting proficiency.

Shipley-2.

The primary study featured one additional cognitive test: Shipley-2 (Shipley et al. 2009).

Shipley-2 is a proprietary test that must be licensed from the publisher for use. As such, it is not a candidate for inclusion in the FPT. However, it is a well-validated brief test of general intelligence. It consists of two subtests: Abstraction (A), which is an assessment of fluid intelligence, and Vocabulary (V ), which is an

Results indicated that subjects who most frequently opted out of impossible questions also showed the best accuracy in their probability judgments for questions with answers. The general knowledge questionnaire also provides a brief measure of crystallized intelligence, and the confidence ratings also provide a measure of calibration, consistent with the "classical method" of identifying forecasting ability (Colson and Cooke 2018), so this task has three outcome measures.

The original item bank from Bennett and Steyvers (2022) contains 100 total questions, six of which have no factually correct answers. The anchor form will include 24 of the 94 questions with correct answers, as well as all six impossible questions. The AIG procedure will be tested by splitting the remaining 48 of the remaining 70 questions into two separate forms of 24 (Form A and Form B). Form A and Form B will each be supplemented with six newly generated impossible questions, generated by feeding the original six impossible questions into ChatGPT. 19 There will be three primary scoring measures from the Impossible Question task: 1.

Proportion of impossible questions opted out of (Impossible Question-Impossible scores, IQI) Denominator Neglect. Denominator neglect is a known decision bias in which people rate proportions that are smaller as being superior if they have larger absolute values in the numerator (Stanovich and West 2008). For example, a 9 in 100 chance to win a lottery will be rated as superior to a chance of 1 in 10.

Additional research has shown that visual arrays in addition to verbal representations reduce denominator neglect (Garcia-Retamero et al. 2010).

Our version of the task is framed as a choice between two boxes with a mixture of gold and silver coins.

Selecting a gold coin is considered a success, and a silver coin a failure.

There are two versions of the task: one in which each option is either represented by only a verbal description or a visual representation (with each option randomized in each trial), and one where each option is represented by both a verbal description and visual representation. In each case, the small denominator lottery is based on a denominator of 10, with the proportion of successes being 10%, 20%, or 30%. The large denominator lottery is based on a denominator of 400, with the proportions ranging from plus or minus 1% to 8% of the small lottery (in intervals of 1%), for a total of 48 trials per version. The primary Denominator Neglect Score (DN) is the total number of cases in which the larger proportion is selected.

We may also consider harmony (the larger denominator option is also the larger proportion) vs conflict (the 19 See Appendix D or https://chat.openai.com/share/40bed4f5-0698-4d51-b003-e2c1f76b39ff for the prompt and questions generated.

larger denominator option is the smaller option) trial types, as well as comparisons involving differences in available information (verbal and visual) in exploratory analyses.

Bayesian Updating. This task is based on work by Phillips and Edwards (1966) version, the sequence of draws will be fixed, while in the AIG version, they will be randomized. A.1. Question Selection Pilot Studies

The first two Pilot studies (Pilot 1A and 1B) were concerned with identifying a suitable list of forecasting questions to use in the primary study. We began with a database of 6,707 forecasting questions that were used in previous forecasting studies known as the autocast dataset (Zou et al. 2022). To reduce these to a more manageable starting point, we first manually filtered the items along several criteria:

Appendix C: Cognitive Test Details

For tests with extensive support in the forecasting literature and psychometric validation, we include only brief summaries and direct interested readers to the references. For the tests with less support in prior forecasting research or newly developed tests, we provide considerably more details.

The Cognitive Reflection Test (CRT).

The CRT is a measure of numerical reasoning ability in the face of an intuitively appealing distractor (Frederick 2005 , Atanasov and Himmelstein 2023 , Himmelstein et al. 2021 , Ho et al. 2024 , Mellers et al. 2015a). We used a 7-item version of the CRT (Toplak et al. 2014).

The Berlin Numeracy Scale (BN). BN is a 4-item measure of general numerical reasoning ability (Cokely et al. 2012 , Atanasov and Himmelstein 2023 , Himmelstein et al. 2021 , Ho et al. 2024 , Mellers et al. 2015a).

Matrix Reasoning (MR).

MR is a visual pattern matching test, variants of which are used in many standardized general intelligence tests (Raven 2000 , Atanasov and Himmelstein 2023 , Himmelstein et al. 2021 , Ho et al. 2024 , Mellers et al. 2015a). The version of matrix reasoning we used has an established AIG version, from which we drew 42 procedurally generated items which were fixed for all participants (Matzen et al. 2010).

Number Series (NS).

NS is a 9-item numerical pattern matching test (Dieckmann et al. 2017 , Atanasov and Himmelstein 2023 , Himmelstein et al. 2021).

The Coherence Forecasting Scale (CFS).

The CFS is a measure of how well people adhere to the logic of probability in their judgments. It is made up of 11 testlets which each involve 3-5 separate components.

CFS has an established AIG version made up of parallel item sets, from which we selected a single set which was fixed for all participants (Ho et al. 2024).

Time Series (TS).

TS is modified from experimental designs that require participants to extrapolate from time series graphs using a visual point-and-click interface (De Baets and Vanderheyden 2021, Reimers and Harvey 2011). For each item, participants see historical data from an unnamed time series that was generated by an underlying function with added Gaussian noise. They are required to predict the subsequent six points in the trend. The test is a 2 (noise level: high or low) x 2 (direction: ascending or descending) x 2 (type: linear or exponential) x 2 (number of historical datapoints: 10 or 30) design. Scores are computed as the mean squared deviation from the generative function.

There were two different versions of TS: an AIG version, in which the historical data was generated independently for each trial, and an anchor version, in which the historical data was fixed for each combination of noise, direction, type, and number of historical datapoints.

Though similar tasks have been used in forecasting research, the context has typically been to study population-level behaviors, rather than measure forecasting proficiency. The intuition behind the inclusion of TS is that using visual information to extrapolate trends is a plausible skill that could benefit forecasters' accuracy.

Leapfrog (LF). LF is a tractable bandit-style task designed to measure optimal exploration vs. exploitation tendencies (Knox et al. 2012). It consists of a set of binary choices between two options. To start, one option is worth 0 points and the other is worth 10 points. Each time a choice is selected, its point value is

Appendix G: Scoring Rules for Cognitive Tasks

What follows is information about the specific scoring rules for the time series, leapfrog, impossible question, denominator neglect, and Bayesian updating tasks. All other scoring rules can be found in references associated with each task.

Time Series. This task is based on work by De Baets and Vanderheyden (2021) and Reimers and Harvey (2011) in which people are asked to extrapolate from graphical time series using a visual point-and-click interface. On each trial, participants will see historical data from an unnamed time series, generated by an underlying function with added noise, and be required to predict the subsequent five points in the trend. The task is a 2 (noise level: high vs low) x 2 (direction: ascending or descending) x 2 (type: linear or exponential) x 2 (historical data: 10 points or 30 points) design, with one item for each combination per form, and two forms (a 16 item anchor form and a 16 item AIG form).

Primary Time Series Scores (TS) are computed as the Mean Squared Deviation from the generative function. Other potential scoring options include proneness to known biases such as trend damping and adding noise, which may be included in exploratory analyses. Exploratory analyses will also compare the effectiveness of scores from different conditions in terms of how well they predict accuracy.

Leapfrog. The leapfrog task is a tractable bandit-style task designed to measure optimal explore vs exploit tendencies (Knox et al. 2012). The task is a set of binary choices between two options. To start, one option is worth 0 points and the other is worth 10 points. Each time a choice is selected, its point value is revealed, but the alternative not chosen remains hidden. After each choice, there is a certain probability (volatility rate) whichever option was worth fewer points will "leapfrog" the option worth more points and increase by 20. So if option A is originally 10 and option B 0, at some point option B will become 20 while A remains 10. Then A will eventually become 30 while B stays at 20. The goal is to select the option worth more points as frequently as possible. The Anchor form will consist of 200 trials in which the option values are pre-determined based on a random set of outcomes with a volatility rate of 12.5%. The AIG form will be a randomly generated series with an equivalent volatility rate and number of trials. Primary Leapfrog Scores (LF) are based on the proportion of trials in which participants selected the higher option. There are also several model based assessments of optimal decision making which can be explored (see Knox et al. 2012).

Impossible Question Criterion.

The impossible question criterion is a task developed by Bennett and Steyvers (2022) (see also Bereby-Meyer et al. 2003). In this task, subjects are given a series of general knowledge problems with two possible answers. For the majority of questions, one of the two options is factually correct, and the other is not. For a minority of questions, there is no factually correct answer. For each question, participants can choose to opt out or answer the question. Participants also provide confidence ratings when they do not opt out.

For example, a question with a factually correct answer is Greenhouse effect refers to: a. Gasses in the atmosphere that trap heat (correct) b. Impact to the earth's ozone layer A question without a factually correct answer is Scoring Rule: )︃⃓ ⃓ ⃓ ⃓ = Absolute Error of log odds on trial j Person level scores will then be computed as Bayesian Update Source Score (BAS) = mean(le j,easy ) and Bayesian Update Forecast Score (BAF) = mean(le j,hard ) for each person.

Appendix I: Disattenuated Correlation Matrix

A disattenuated correlation matrix can be broken into three parts. The lower triangle contains raw Pearson correlations between pairs of variables. The diagonal contains Cronbach's α for each variable. The upper triange contains *disattenuated* correlations, which uses the α coefficients to provide an estimate the correlation between variables if they were completely free of measurement error. intervals (P = .25 and P = .75 forecasts), and 90% prediction interval (P = .05 and P = .95 forecasts).

They also include an annotation for the sample standardized S-score for that forecast, and vertical lines representing the question resolution value. Standardization One shortcoming of sample standardization is that it requires a large number of forecasters to estimate the sample statistics Āk and S A k . For questions with historical time series, such as those used in this study, an alternative is to standardize prior to scoring for accuracy based on historical information. For each such forecasting question, where available (see supplementary materials), we recorded historical trends at monthly intervals from December 2022 through April 2024.

2.

Proportion of correct responses to possible questions (Impossible Question-General Knowledge scores, IQG) 3. Overconfidence/calibration on possible questions, calculated as mean(Confidence) -IQG (Impossible Question-Overconfidence scores, IQO) in which people must iteratively update their probabilistic judgments based on new information.

In this task, two boxes are displayed, one containing p red balls and 100 -p blue balls. The other contains p blue balls and 100 -p red balls (in our version, p is either 60 or 70). One of the boxes is chosen at random with equal probability. Five balls are drawn one at a time with replacement. The task has two versions. In the Source version, after each drawn ball, subjects must report the probability that the box is the majority red or majority blue box. In the Forecast version, subjects must report the probability the next draw will be a blue ball. In the anchor j = Number of successes = Number of blue balls drawn through draw j f j = Number of failures = Number of red balls drawn through draw j L j = Likelihood Ratio given data after draw j = (︃ P (Blue) blue urn P (Blue) red urn )︃ s j -f j Ω 1j = LΩ0 = Posterior Odds of blue urn after draw j For source (easy) version: pj,easy = subjects' reported probability of reference (blue) urn after draw jError of log odds on trial j For forecast (hard) version pj,hard = subjects' reported probability of blue ball after draw j u j = Ω 1j 1 + Ω 1j true probability of blue urn after draw j p j,hard = u j P (Blue) + (1 -u j ) (1 -P (Blue)) = true probability of blue ball on next draw

Figure I1 Disattenuated Correlation Matrix of Criterion and Predictor Variables

All correlations are absolute values (magnitudes) for consistency. For M SAn, the largest Cronbach's α values across order conditions was used, which is the most conservative value for the purposes of disattenuation. Reliability was not calculated for predictor M SAn to avoid complications involving repeated elicitation of Block X. For the calibration metric of the Impossible Question Criterion reliability was not calculable. In cases where there was no reliability calculated, it was treated as having a value of 1, meaning for correlations above the diagonal are raw rather than disattenuated. Hist. = historically standardized M SAn, Samp. = sample standardized M SAn, Crit. = Criterion, Pred. = Predictor, Ord. Brier = Ordinal Brier 21 (c) as Ω 1j = L c Ω 0 , and | log(c)| as an alternative measure of incoherence.

Appendix H: Data and Code Availability All data, code used to process the raw data, and code used to program cognitive tests 22 are publicly at https://github.com/forecastingresearch/fpt. All Qualtrics survey files containing forecasting questions and data analysis code can be found in the supplementary materials at https://osf.io/q2e94/? view_only= 73cacdfbc12b4e60adbc220e5341d661. All data analyses were conducted in R version 4.3.0 (R Core Team 2023) using the following packages: tidyverse (Wickham et al. 2019), lme4 (Bates et al. 2015), lavaan (Rosseel 2012), pROC (Robin et al. 2011), emmeans (Lenth 2023), domir (Luchman 2024), and chemometrics (Filzmoser 2023).

Appendix J: Response Times for QEF QuestionsThe median response time for QEF questions in Wave 1 was 3.24 minutes, IQR = [1.87, 6.07]. The median response time for QEF questions in Wave 7 was 1.92, IQR = [0.94, 3.79]. FigureJ1shows distributions of response times by item and wave.

The terms fixed-variable format and probability elicitation format are interchangeable, as are the terms fixedprobability format and quantile elicitation format. In each case, the former term refers to the features of the probability distribution held constant by the elicitation, while the latter term refers to the features of the probability distribution the forecaster is required to report. To avoid confusion, we will use the terms probability elicitation format (PEF) and quantile elicitation format (QEF) throughout.

The primary study was preregistered on the Open Science Foundation. Preregistration and supplementary materials can be found at https://osf.io/q2e94/? view_only=73cacdfbc12b4e60adbc220e5341d661

For both Brier and S-scores, less than 1% of forecasts produces standardized scores with magnitudes greater than five. However, the largest of these for Brier scores was 7.30, while the largest for S-scores was 58.16, so Winsorization was designed to address unwanted leverage of a small number of observations, some of which likely reflected input errors rather than poor judgment(Zhu et al. 2026), rather than quantity of outliers.

The logic for this is the same as for Winsorizing sample standardized scores, in that it is more a concern about leverage of extreme outliers than the quantity of outliers, though in this case, 9.1% of forecasts were Winsorized.

Note that we use scaled rather than standardized accuracy so that question effects can vary as a function of time.

We used DN-C and BU-H (excluding DN-S and BU-E) to reduce redundancy and excluded the Shipley-2 subscales since they are proprietary and cannot be used in the final FPT.

Given our large sample size, we focus on effect size rather than statistical significance, as even extremely small effects proved statistically significant.

MR and CFS have previously validated AIG versions(Matzen et al. 2010 , Ho et al. 2024).

Scores were reversed to conform to traditional testing conventions where higher, rather than lower scores reflect better performance.

Note that some of these definitions only work under our specific setup where certain conditions are met, e.g. the urns have exactly inverse probabilities.

Conservatism, as 0 < c < 1 is the modal empirical finding.
